## Page 1


Coarse Menger property of quasi-minor excluded graphs
and length spaces
Chun-Hung Liu∗
Department of Mathematics,
Texas A&M University,
College Station, TX 77843-3368, USA
May 12, 2026
Abstract
Menger’s theorem is an important building block of numerous results in the study
of graph structure. We consider a variant in terms of coarse geometry. We say that
a set of graphs has the weak coarse Menger property if there exist functions f and g
such that for any graph G in this set, subsets X and Y of vertices of G, and positive
integers k and r, either there exist k paths between X and Y pairwise at distance
at least r, or there exists a union of at most f(k, r) balls of radius at most g(k, r)
intersecting all paths between X and Y . Nguyen, Scott and Seymour proved that the
set of all graphs does not have the weak coarse Menger property and asked whether
every proper minor-closed family of finite graphs has it.
In this paper, we provide a positive answer to this question in a stronger form:
it is true for the set of locally finite graphs with an excluded finite minor, and the
functions f and g can be chosen so that f only depends on the number k of the paths
in the packing and the function g is a linear function of the distance threshold r and is
independent of k, which is optimal up to a constant factor. Our result extends to every
length space quasi-isometric to a locally finite graph or metric graph with an excluded
finite minor, such as complete Riemannian surfaces of finite Euler genus, string graphs,
and Cayley graphs of finitely generated minor-excluded groups.
1
Introduction
Menger’s theorem, arguably one of the most important theorems in graph theory, states
that for any graph1 G and subsets X and Y of the set of vertices, the maximum number
of disjoint paths in G between X and Y equals the minimum size of a set of vertices in G
intersecting all paths between X and Y . It is not only a ubiquitous building block in the
∗Email:chliu@tamu.edu. Partially supported by NSF under CAREER award DMS-2144042.
1Graphs in this paper are finite unless otherwise specified. Locally finite graphs can be finite or infinite.
1
arXiv:2605.10068v1  [math.CO]  11 May 2026


## Page 2


study of graph structure but also shows the optimal duality between packing and covering
for paths between two prescribed sets. The packing problem and the covering problem are
two of the most fundamental problems in combinatorial optimization. The former asks for
the maximum number of pairwise disjoint“interesting objects” contained in the host space;
the latter asks for the minimum size of a set of “basic elements” intersecting all “interesting
objects”. In this paper we study an analog of packing and covering along the line of coarse
geometry. More precisely, we study an analog of Menger’s theorem by addressing packing
and covering paths in graphs or length spaces coarsely.
Coarse geometry or large-scale geometry studies properties of metric spaces involving
large distance. For example, a single point and a ball of small radius can be considered as
“equivalent” when we only consider large-scale behaviors. Hence the large-scale analog of a
packing of disjoint objects is a set of objects that are pairwise far apart, and the large-scale
analog of a covering is a set of balls of bounded radius intersecting all interesting objects.
(Finite or infinite) graphs can be treated as metric spaces since we can define distance
between any two vertices. Formally, for any two vertices x and y of a (finite or infinite)
graph G, we define distG(x, y) to be the infimum length of a path in G between x and y,
where the length of a path is the number of its edges. This function distG is a metric on the
set V (G) of vertices of G as long as G is connected.
In spite of being special cases of metric spaces, graphs play an important role in the
study of general metric spaces. For example, Gromov [23] introduced asymptotic dimension
of metric spaces to study finitely generated groups via studying asymptotic dimension of
their Cayley graphs; Yu [48] used this notion to prove that the Novikov higher signature
conjecture holds for manifolds whose fundamental group has finite asymptotic dimension.
Asymptotic dimension is a large-scale analog of topological dimension of topological spaces
and is a large-scale analog of chromatic number of graphs. The chromatic number of a graph
G is the infimum k such that its set of vertices can be written as a union of at most k
sets of pairwise non-adjacent vertices (i.e. k sets each consisting of balls of radius 0 that
are pairwise at distance greater than 1). The asymptotic dimension of a metric space is the
infimum k such that for every r > 0, its set of points can be written as a union of at most
k sets X1, ..., Xk, where each Xi is a union of sets Xi,1, Xi,2, ... that are pairwise at distance
greater than r such that each Xi,j is contained in a ball of radius bounded in terms of r.
After a sequence of papers [18, 28, 7, 31, 6] about asymptotic dimension of surfaces and
minor-closed families of graphs appeared, several groups of scholars independently consid-
ered very similar questions about large-scale analogs of known results in graph theory. In
particular, a natural conjecture (Conjecture 1.1) that is a large-scale analog of Menger’s
theorem was proposed independently by Albrechtsen, Huynh, Jacobs, Knappe and Wollan
[4] and Georgakopoulos and Papasoglu [21].
Before stating Conjecture 1.1, we introduce some notions to simplify the description.
Let G be a (finite or infinite) graph. Let X and Y be (not necessarily disjoint) subsets
of V (G). An X-Y -path in G is a path that has at least one end in X and at least one end
in Y . Note that every vertex in X ∩Y forms an X-Y path. Moreover, some internal vertex
of an X-Y path might be in X ∪Y .
We shall find a packing of pairwise far apart X-Y paths. For any sets S, T ⊆V (G), we
define distG(S, T) = infs∈S,t∈T distG(s, t). The distance between two subgraphs of G is defined
to be the distance between their vertex-sets.

We will cover X-Y paths by using balls of bounded radius. Let S ⊆V (G), and let k and
r be real numbers. For every vertex x ∈V (G), we define distG(x, S) = infs∈S distG(x, s).
We define N ≤r
G [S] = {v ∈V (G) : distG(v, S) ≤r}; note that N ≤r
G [S] is the union of the
balls of radius at most r centered at points in S. A set Z ⊆V (G) is (k, r)-centered in G if
Z ⊆N ≤r
G [W] for some W ⊆V (G) with |W| ≤k. Note that every (k, r)-centered set in G is
a set contained in a union of at most k balls of radius at most r.
Conjecture 1.1 ([4, Conjecture 3],[21, Conjecture 1.4]). There exists a real number c > 0
such that for any graph G, subsets X, Y of V (G) and positive integers k, r, either G contains
k X-Y paths pairwise at distance at least r, or there exists a set (k −1, cr)-centered in G
intersecting all X-Y paths.
The case k = 2 with arbitrary r of Conjecture 1.1 was independently proved by Albrecht-
sen et al. [4] and Georgakopoulos and Papasoglu [21]. However, Nguyen, Scott and Seymour
[34] disproved the case k = r = 3 of Conjecture 1.1 even when G has maximum degree at
most 3. Their counterexamples also disprove the cases for any pair (k, r) with k ≥3 and
r ≥3 and imply that Conjecture 1.1 remains false even if the bound cr for the radius of
balls is changed to any function of k and r.
On the other hand, the cases r = 2 with arbitrary k ≥3 of Conjecture 1.1 remain open. If
the graph G has bounded maximum degree, then every ball of radius cr contains a bounded
number of vertices, so every (k−1, cr)-centered set is a set of bounded number of vertices not
far away from k −1 particular vertices. Gartland, Korhonen, Lokshtanov [20] and Hendrey,
Norin, Steiner and Turcotte [24] independently proved the r = 2 case of Conjecture 1.1 if
G has bounded maximum degree and we only require the hitting set to have bounded size
but do not require the vertices in the hitting set to be not far away from k −1 particular
vertices.
Some results related to the restriction of Conjecture 1.1 to minor-closed families are
known. Nguyen, Scott and Seymour [37] pointed out that the tree-width2 of the counterex-
amples to Conjecture 1.1 in [34] is at most 6, showing that Conjecture 1.1 remains false even
if we restrict it to some minor-closed families with simple structure and allow the bound for
the radius of balls to be dependent on both k and r. On the other hand, they [37] proved
that Conjecture 1.1 holds for graphs of bounded path-width if we allow the bound cr for
the radius of balls to be a function of k, r and the bound for the path-width. In addition,
Nguyen, Scott and Seymour [38] conjectured that Conjecture 1.1 is true if we restrict it to
graphs embeddable in a fixed surface Σ and allow the bound for the radius to be a function
of k, r, Σ; they [38] proved the case of this weaker conjecture when G can be embedded in
the plane such that all vertices in X ∪Y are incident with the infinite face. We remark that,
unlike the original form of Conjecture 1.1, if the radius of balls is allowed to be dependent
on both k and r, then it is unclear whether at least k −1 balls are still required.
Nguyen, Scott and Seymour [34] proposed the following weakening of the false Conjecture
1.1 by relaxing the number of balls and the radius of balls in the hitting set.
2We do not need the formal definition of tree-width and path-width in this paper. Graphs of bounded
tree-width and bounded path-width are the graphs that have a tree-decomposition and a path-decomposition,
respectively, with bounded bag size. Tree-decomposition and path-decomposition are defined in Section 3.4.

## Page 4


Conjecture 1.2 ([34, Conjecture 4.2]). There exist functions f, g : N →R such that for
any graph G, subsets X, Y of V (G) and positive integers k, r, either G contains k X-Y paths
pairwise at distance at least r, or there exists an (f(k, r), g(k, r))-centered set intersecting
all X-Y paths.
More recently, Nguyen, Scott and Seymour [36] further disproved Conjecture 1.2. Because
their counterexamples contain arbitrary graphs as a minor, they asked the following question:
Question 1.3 ([36, Section 3]). Does Conjecture 1.2 hold for graphs in a fixed proper minor-
closed family?
A graph H is a minor of another graph G if H is isomorphic to a graph that can be
obtained from G by repeatedly deleting a vertex or an edge or contracting an edge. A minor-
closed family is a set F of graphs such that for every graph G in F, every minor of G is also
in F. A minor-closed family is proper if it does not contain all graphs. Proper minor-closed
families are general. In particular, collecting the graphs that satisfy any graph property
that is preserved under vertex-deletion, edge-deletion and edge-contraction gives a proper
minor-closed family. Typical examples include, but are not limited to, the set of graphs that
can be drawn in a fixed surface with no crossing, the set of linkless embeddable graphs, the
set of knotless embeddable graphs, the set of the graphs whose Colin de Verdi`ere parameter
is at most a fixed constant, and the sets of graphs of bounded tree-width or path-width.
Question 1.3 cannot be relaxed to many natural graph classes that are more general than
minor-closed families, such as:
• Graph classes of asymptotic dimension or Assouad-Nagata dimension at most 1: The
asymptotic dimension of a graph class is defined to be the asymptotic dimension of
the (possibly infinite) graph that is the union of disjoint copies of all graphs in this
class. The asymptotic dimension of any proper minor-closed family is at most 2 [6, 31].
This result was later generalized to Assouad-Nagata dimension of proper minor-closed
families [14, 30]. The Assouad-Nagata dimension addresses both large-scale and small-
scale behaviors of the space and is at least the asymptotic dimension. The Assouad-
Nagata dimension of classes of graphs of bounded tree-width is at most 1 [14, 30].
An infinite class of counterexamples of Conjecture 1.2 provided in [36] (called (ℓ, m)-
blocks) has the property that for every graph G in this class, there exists an integer ℓ
such that G is obtained from a graph W of tree-width at most 2 with all components
having radius at most 2ℓ+ 1 by adding internally disjoint paths of length 2ℓ+ 1
between vertices in W.
It is straightforward to show that this class has Assouad-
Nagata dimension (and hence asymptotic dimension) at most 1 by using the fact that
bounded tree-width graphs have Assouad-Nagata dimension at most 1.
• General proper topological minor-closed classes: A graph H is a topological minor of
another graph G if some graph obtained by subdividing edges of H is isomorphic to a
subgraph of G. Every topological minor of G is a minor of G. Another infinite class of
counterexamples of Conjecture 1.2 provided in [36] has the property that every graph
in this class has exactly one vertex of degree greater than 3. Every graph that has at
most one vertex of degree greater than 3 does not contain any graph that has at least
two vertices of degree greater than 3 as a topological minor. Hence Conjecture 1.2
4


## Page 5


does not hold for any topological minor-closed family that does not forbid any graph
with at most one vertex of degree greater than 3. Note that Question 1.3 is equivalent
to asking whether Conjecture 1.2 holds for every topological minor-closed family that
forbids at least one graph that has maximum degree at most 3.
• Graph classes that forbid a fat minor: Fat minors is a coarse version of graph minors
implicitly introduced by Fujiwara and Papasoglu in [18] when studying asymptotic
dimension of geodesic metric spaces homeomorphic to R2 and explicitly defined by
Bonamy et al. [6, 7] when studying Assouad-Nagata dimension of graphs embedded in
surfaces. A graph contains another graph H as a minor if and only if it contains H as
a 1-fat minor; if a graph contains H as a k-fat minor, then it contains H as a k′-fat
minor for every 0 ≤k′ ≤k. Albrechtsen and Davies [1] constructed counterexamples
to Conjecture 1.2 not containing the (154 × 154)-grid as a 3-fat minor by modifying
the counterexamples in [36].
1.1
Main result (the basic form)
The first main result of this paper is Theorem 1.4, which provides a positive answer to
Question 1.3 in the following stronger form: First, our bound g for the radius of balls in the
hitting set is a linear function of r independent of k. If we combine Theorem 1.4 and a result
in [12], then we can further remove the dependency of r from the function f for the number of
balls (see Theorem 1.12), which will be optimal since the number of the balls must depend on
the number of objects k in the packing, and the radius of balls must depend on the distance
threshold r between the objects in the packing. As far as we know, all known positive results
that are true for all r ≥1 related to the false Conjecture 1.2 in the literature require the
radius of balls to be dependent on both k and r, except those on specific graph classes that
can be easily proved via a special case of Proposition 1.8 for graphs of bounded tree-width.
Second, our result holds if we pack and cover X-Y paths whose ends are far apart, which can
be restated in terms of the coarse Erd˝os-P´osa property for rooted fat K2-minors and imply
a result about packing and covering A-paths coarsely in minor-closed families. Third, our
result holds for locally finite infinite graphs, which allows us to extend our result to some
continuous spaces such as Riemannian surfaces via quasi-isometry (see Section 1.2).
For a graph G, subsets X, Y ⊆V (G) and a real number ℓ≥0, an (ℓ, X, Y )-path in
G is an X-Y path in G such that distG(a, b) ≥ℓ, where a and b are its ends. Note that
(0, X, Y )-paths are exactly X-Y paths. Since every proper minor-closed family is a subset
of the set of H-minor free graphs for some graph H, the ℓ= 0 case of the following theorem
answers Question 1.3 affirmatively.
Theorem 1.4. For every (finite) graph H, there exist a function f : N2 × N0 →R and an
integer cH such that if G is an H-minor free locally finite graph, then for any subsets X, Y
of V (G) and integers k, r ∈N and ℓ∈N0, either G contains k (ℓ, X, Y )-paths pairwise at
distance in G at least r, or there exists a set (f(k, r, ℓ), cHr + ℓ)-centered in G intersecting
all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths in G.
We did not put effort to optimize f and cH in Theorem 1.4. Our proof of Theorem 1.4
shows that the integer cH satisfies the following:
5


## Page 6


• cH ≤8γH+44, where γH is the maximum nonnegative integer such that Kmax{|V (H)|+1,5}
cannot be embedded in some surface of Euler genus at most γH,
• If G is finite and H is planar, then the bound cHr + ℓcan be replaced by r/2,
• If G is finite and H is non-planar, then cH ≤4γH + 22, where γH is the maximum
nonnegative integer such that H cannot be embedded in some surface of Euler genus
at most γH,
• If G is finite and H is an apex-graph, then cH ≤14.
A graph is an apex-graph if one can delete at most one vertex to make it planar. For every
surface Σ, Euler’s formula implies that every graph embeddable in Σ is K3,2g+3-minor free,
where g is the Euler genus of Σ. Since K3,2g+3 is an apex-graph, it implies that the integer cH
in Theorem 1.4 for graphs embeddable in Σ can be chosen to be 14 so that it is independent
of the surface Σ. Blaˇzej, Pilipczuk and Protopapas [5] independently proved that cH can
be chosen to be 1 for finite graphs G embedded in a surface Σ with ℓ= 0; it is unknown
whether cH = 1 is optimal for this special case, and their result is for this special case only
and does not apply to general proper minor-closed families. Apex-minor free graphs are
beyond graphs embedded in surfaces of bounded Euler genus. For example, if H is obtained
from disjoint copies of K3,3 by identifying one vertex from each copy into a vertex, then H
is an apex-graph, but there exists no single surface such that all H-minor free graphs can be
embedded.
We remark that the bound for the radius of balls must grow at least as fast as linear
in r if the number of balls f is independent of r: Consider the (r × n)-grid, where n is an
arbitrarily large integer. Let X be the first column and Y be the last column of this grid.
Then there are no two X-Y paths pairwise at distance at least r since the distance between
any two distinct vertices in X is at most r −1. On the other hand, to hit all X-Y paths,
every row must be intersected by a ball. Every ball with radius s can only intersect at most
2s + 1 ≤3s rows. So s ≥r/3f.
Theorem 1.4 achieves the linear bound in r for the radius of balls, but the number of
balls f depends on both k and r. Instead of putting effort to remove the dependency of r
from f by using a more complicated proof, in Section 1.2 we will show that by combining
Theorem 1.4 with a simple trick about scaling (Lemma 9.5) and a result of Davies, Distel and
Hickingbotham [12] announced in [11] about quasi-isometry, we can remove the dependency
of r from f while keeping the bound for the radius linear in r (see Theorem 1.12). So our
bound for the radius is optimal up to a constant factor.
We state Theorem 1.4 and Theorem 1.12 separately for a few reasons. First, Theorem
1.4 is the key for this argument and its proof does not rely on any recent result. Second,
Theorem 1.12 only works for some ℓ. Third, the integer cH in Theorem 1.4 is more explicit.
The explicit integer cH could be beneficial for studying some concrete minor-closed families.
For example, linkless embeddable graphs and knotless embeddable graphs are K6-minor free
[9, 47] and K7-minor free [9], respectively, so cH can be chosen to be 22 and 30, respectively
(when G is finite), and to be 60 and 68, respectively (when G is locally finite).
Theorem 1.4 can also be applied to a conjecture of Geelen about A-paths. For a (finite
or infinite) graph G and a subset A of V (G), an A-path is a path in G between two distinct
6


## Page 7


vertices in A. Gallai [19] proved an analog of Menger’s theorem for packing and covering
A-paths: for any graph G, A ⊆V (G) and k ∈N, either G contains k disjoint A-paths, or
there exists Z ⊆V (G) with |Z| ≤2k −2 intersecting all A-paths. Jim Geelen proposed the
following conjecture on a coarse version of Gallai’s theorem:
Conjecture 1.5 (Geelen). 3 There exist functions f, g : N →R such that for any k, r ∈N,
graph G and A ⊆V (G), either there exist k A-paths pairwise at distance in G at least r, or
there exists a set (f(k), g(r))-centered in G intersecting all A-paths.
Note that (1, A, A)-paths are exactly A-paths. So Theorem 1.4 immediately implies the
following partial result for Conjecture 1.5 for proper minor-closed families.
Corollary 1.6. For every (finite) graph H, there exist a function f : N3 →R and an integer
cH such that if G is an H-minor free locally finite graph, then for any subset A of V (G) and
integers k, r, ℓ∈N, either G contains k (ℓ, A, A)-paths pairwise at distance at least r, or
there exists a set (f(k, r, ℓ), cHr + ℓ)-centered in G intersecting all (ℓ, A, A)-paths in G.
The integer cH in Corollary 1.6 is the same as the integer cH in Theorem 1.4. In particular,
cH = 14 when H is an apex-graph and G is finite. Moreover, we can further remove the
dependency of r from f by using Lemma 9.5 and a result in [12] (see Theorem 1.12).
We remark that Hickingbotham and Joret [26] solved the r = 2 case of Conjecture 1.5
with (f(k), g(2)) = (78k −78, 1) and with (f(k), g(2)) = (4k −4, 4).
When this paper
was under preparation, Distel, Giocanti, Hodor, Legrand-Duchesne and Micek [15] proved a
weaker version of Conjecture 1.5 for all r with (f(k), g(k, r)) = (4k −4, 256kr). The result in
[15] is incomparable with the ℓ= 1 and finite graph case of Corollary 1.6 since their bound
g(k, r) for the radius of balls depends on both k and r.
One can restate X-Y paths and A-paths in terms of rooted K2-minors and restate

Corollary 1.7 is a large-scale and rooted fat minors analog of a classical result in [39]
about the Erd˝os-P´osa property for minor models. We remark that Corollary 1.7 cannot be
generalized to rooted (fat or not) P3-minor models with three distinct roots, where P3 is the
3-vertex path: take a w × w-grid for an arbitrarily large w, let R1 be the first column, R2 be
the first row, and R3 be the last column, then it is easy to see that there do not exist two
disjoint (R1, R2, R3)-rooted P3-minor models, but any set intersecting all (R1, R2, R3)-rooted
P3-minor models requires size Ω(w) and hence requires an unbounded number of balls as
grids have maximum degree 4. This example shows that if G contains an arbitrarily large
grid, then rooted P3-minor models cannot satisfy the coarse Erd˝os-P´osa property. We can
show that the large grids are essentially the only obstructions for the coarse Erd˝os-P´osa
property for packing and covering rooted minor model models, as follows:
Proposition 1.8. For any (finite) planar graph H and (finite) graph W, there exists β ∈N
such that if G is an H-minor free (finite) graph, then for any collection (Rv : v ∈V (W)) of
subsets of V (G) and integers k, r, ℓ∈N, either G contains k (Rv : v ∈V (W))-rooted ℓ-fat
W-minor models pairwise at distance at least r, or there exists a set (βk, max{⌈r−1

Proposition 1.8 is a simple corollary of a lemma in this paper (Lemma 3.6) developed on
the way of proving Theorem 1.4. Some special cases of Proposition 1.8 are probably folklore.
Our Lemma 3.6 in fact deals with a setting that is more general than Proposition 1.8.
1.2
Weak coarse Menger property and quasi-isometry
In this section we extend Theorem 1.4 to more general metric spaces and remove depen-
dency of r from the number of balls f.
A length space is a metric space (X, d) such that for any x, y ∈X and ϵ > 0, there
exists a path in X from x to y with length at most d(x, y) + ϵ; here a path from x to y is a
rectifiable curve from x to y (i.e. a continuous function from [0, 1] to X such that f(0) = x
and f(1) = y). Note that a graph is not a length space since the notion of paths in graphs
is different from the one in length spaces. However, the properties considered in this paper
(i.e. paths that are pairwise far apart and unions of balls intersecting paths) are irrelevant
to this difference.
Let (X, dX) be a length space, and let A, B be subsets of X. For ℓ≥0, an (ℓ, A, B)-path
in (X, dX) is a path from a to b for some a, b ∈X such that dX(a, b) ≥ℓ. For any α, β ≥0,
we say that a set Z ⊆X is (α, β)-centered in dX if Z is contained in the union of at most α
balls of radius at most β.
We say that a (finite or infinite) graph or a length space (X, dX) has the weak coarse
Menger property if there exist functions f, g : N×R>0 →R such that for any k ∈N, r ∈R>0
and subsets A, B of X, either there exist k paths in (X, dX) from A to B pairwise at distance
in dX at least r, or there exists Z ⊆X such that Z is (f(k, r), g(k, r))-centered and intersects
every path in (X, dX) from A to B.
8


## Page 9


Quasi-isometry is an equivalence relation that preserves several coarse properties, includ-
ing the weak coarse Menger property. (See Lemma 9.1 for precise statements.) Formally,
for real numbers m ≥1 and a ≥0, an (m, a)-quasi-isometry from a metric space (X, dX) to
another metric space (Y, dY ) is a function ι : X →Y such that
•
1
m · dX(s, t) −a ≤dY (ι(s), ι(t)) ≤m · dX(s, t) + a for any s, t ∈X, and
• for every y ∈Y , there exists x ∈X such that dY (ι(x), y) ≤a.
We say that (X, dX) is quasi-isometry to (Y, dY ) if there exists an (m′, a′)-quasi-isometry
from (X, dX) to (Y, dY ) for some m′ ≥1 and a′ ≥0.
Hence Theorem 1.4 can be generalized to other classes of graphs and length spaces via
quasi-isometry. Several interesting length spaces are known to be quasi-isometric to H-minor
free (locally finite) graphs. For example, Davies [11] proved the following two results:
Theorem 1.9 ([11, Theorem 1.7]). For every nonnegative integer g, there exist m ≥1 and
a ≥0 such that every complete Riemannian surface Σ of Euler genus g is (m, a)-quasi-
isometric to a locally finite graph embeddable in Σ.
Theorem 1.10 ([11, Theorem 1.1]). There exist m ≥1 and a ≥0 such that every string
graph4 is (m, a)-quasi-isometric to a planar graph.
Graphs that are quasi-isometric to graphs of bounded tree-width (i.e. H-minor free graphs
for some planar graph H) have been extensively studied. For example, the following planar
graphs H satisfy that for any fixed ℓ∈N, graphs with no ℓ-fat H-minor are quasi-isometric
to H-minor free graphs: H is a cycle [21], H = K2,t for any fixed t ≥1 [2] (improving earlier
results of [8, 17, 21]) and H = K4 [3] (improving an earlier result of [17]). In addition, graphs
that admit a tree-decomposition whose every bag is (α, β)-centered (for any fixed α, β ≥0)
are quasi-isometric to an H-minor free graph for some planar H [13, 25, 35]; examples of
such graphs include graphs of bounded rank-width, chordal graphs, and graphs of bounded
tree-independence number. Hence Theorem 1.4 implies that those graphs have the weak
coarse Menger property once we apply quasi-isometry. However, we remark that proving
the weak coarse Menger property for those graphs is much easier and does not require the
full strength of Theorem 1.4: it is folklore that graphs that are quasi-isometric to graphs of
bounded tree-width have a tree-decomposition whose every bag is (α, β)-centered for some
α, β ≥0, so our Lemma 3.6 can be directly applied to obtain even stronger results.
We can also extend results from graphs to weighted graphs. For I ⊆R>0, an I-weighted
(finite or infinite) graph is a pair (G, ϕ), where G is a (finite or infinite) graph and ϕ :
E(G) →I is a function. Note that ϕ(e) can be thought as the length of e for every edge
e of G. For every path P in an I-weighted graph (G, ϕ), we define the length of P to be
P
e∈E(P) ϕ(e). It defines a metric dist(G,ϕ) such that for every u, v ∈V (G), dist(G,ϕ)(u, v)
is the infimum length of a path in (G, ϕ) between u and v. Hence (G, ϕ) is a metric space
with metric dist(G,ϕ). Note that every graph is equivalent to a {1}-weighted graph. We say
4A graph is a string graph if it is the intersection graph of path-connected subsets of the plane. That
is, there exists a bijection from the set of vertices to a set of path-connected subsets of the plane such that
two vertices are adjacent in the string graph if and only if the corresponding two path-connected subsets
intersect.
9


## Page 10


that an R>0-weighted graph (G, ϕ) is locally finite if every ball of finite radius contains only
finitely many vertices of G. For every graph H, we say that an R>0-weighted graph (G, ϕ)
is H-minor free if G is H-minor free.
Davies [11, Theorem 1.5] proved that there exist m ≥1 and a ≥0 such that every
(0, 1]-weighted planar graph is (m, a)-quasi-isometric to a planar graph. This result was
generalized to H-minor free (0, 1]-weighted graphs for any fixed graph H in [12] (see [11]),
as follows:
Theorem 1.11 ([12]). For every (finite) graph H, there exist m ≥1 and a ≥0 such
that every H-minor free locally finite (0, 1]-weighted graph is (m, a)-quasi-isometric to an
H-minor free locally finite graph.
Note that I-weighted graphs are not length spaces because they are discrete spaces.
We can make an I-weighted graph a length space by viewing each edge as a topological
space homeomorphic to [0, 1]. More formally, a metric graph is a metric space (X, dX) such
that its underlying space is a 1-dimensional complex that is the union of all edges of a R>0-
weighted graph (G, ϕ), and dX is an extension of dist(G,ϕ) such that for every e = xy ∈E(G),
there exists a homeomorphism fe from e to [0, 1] with fe(x) = 0 and fe(y) = 1 such that
for every z ∈e −{x, y}, there exists tz ∈[0, 1] with fe(z) = tz, dX(x, z) = tzϕ(e) and
dX(y, z) = (1 −tz)ϕ(e); we say that (X, dX) is defined by (G, ϕ) in this case. We say that a
metric graph is locally finite if it is defined by a locally finite R>0-weighted graph. For every
graph H, a metric graph is H-minor free if it is defined by an H-minor free R>0-weighted
graph.
If G is a graph, then it is also a (0, 1]-weighted graph and is (1, 1)-quasi-isometric to the
metric graph defines by it. On the other hand, if G′ is a metric graph defined by a R>0-
weighted graph (G, ϕ), then G′ is not necessarily quasi-isometric to G unless there exists c
such that G is a (0, c]-weighted graph. This does not create a serious issue when studying
the weak coarse Menger property since we can subdivide edges to reduce the length of each
edge. See Section 9 for details. Hence our result can also be applied to metric graphs.
With an additional trick about scaling (Lemma 9.5), we can extend Theorem 1.4 and
Corollary 1.6 (for some ℓ) to more general spaces and remove the dependency of r from the
number of balls, as follows:
Theorem 1.12. Let H be a (finite) graph, and let m ≥1, a ≥0 be real numbers. Let F be
the set consisting of
1. the locally finite H-minor free R>0-weighted graphs,
2. the locally finite H-minor free metric graphs, and
3. the length spaces that are (m, a)-quasi-isometric to a locally finite H-minor free metric
graph.
Let F′ be the set of length spaces that are (m, a)-quasi-isometric to a locally finite H-minor
free (0, 1]-weighted graph. Then the following hold:
1. There exist a function f : N →R and real numbers c, c′ ≥0 such that if (W, dW) ∈
F ∪F′, then for any X, Y ⊆W, k ∈N and r ∈R>0, either there exist k paths
10


## Page 11


in (W, dW) from X to Y pairwise at distance in dW at least r, or there exists an
(f(k), cr + c′)-centered set in (W, dW) intersecting all paths in (W, dW) from X to Y .
2. There exist a function f : N →R and real numbers c, c′ ≥0 such that if (W, dW) ∈
F ∪F′, then for any A ⊆W, k ∈N and r ∈R>0, either there exist k A-paths in
(W, dW) pairwise at distance in dW at least r, or there exists an (f(k), cr +c′)-centered
set in (W, dW) intersecting all A-paths in (W, dW).
3. For every α ≥0, there exist a function f : N →R and a real number c ≥0 such that
if a = 0 and (W, dW) ∈F, then for any X, Y ⊆W, k ∈N and r ∈R>0, either there
exist k (αr, X, Y )-paths in (W, dW) pairwise at distance in dW at least r, or there exists
an (f(k), cr)-centered set in (W, dW) intersecting all (αr, X, Y )-paths in (W, dW).
Moreover, if a = 0 and (W, dW) ∈F, then the real number c′ in Statements 1 and 2 can be
chosen to be 0.
Note that the dependency in Theorem 1.12 is optimal since the number of balls must
depend on the number k of paths that we pack. Moreover, recall that we showed that the
radius of balls in the hitting set must grow at least as fast as a linear function in the required
distance threshold r for the paths in the packing when the number of balls only depends on
k. So the bound for the radius of balls in Theorem 1.12 is optimal up to a constant factor.
2
Proof sketch and organization of this paper
Theorem 1.4 is the most technical result in this paper. Once Theorem 1.4 is proved,
Theorem 1.12 follows from a combination of results about quasi-isometry and scaling, and
the argument is fairly simple. We show how to derive Theorem 1.12 in Section 9 and then
conclude this paper by introducing other open problems in Section 10. The rest of the paper
is dedicated to the proof of Theorem 1.4. In this section, we sketch a proof of Theorem 1.4
and explain the organization of this paper.
The crucial ingredient of the proof of Theorem 1.4 is the following technical form for
finite graphs, and the motivation for considering this technical form will be clear as soon as
we start sketching its proof. (See Section 3.1 for notation.)
Theorem 2.1. For any graph H and integers k, r ∈N and ℓ, ξ, η ∈N0, there exist ξ∗, η∗∈N
such that the following holds. Let G be an H-minor free graph, and let L be an induced
subgraph of G. Let Z ⊆V (G) be a (ξ, η)-centered set in G such that NG(V (L)) ⊆Z. Let X
and Y be subsets of V (L). If L does not contain k (ℓ, X, Y )-paths in G pairwise at distance
in G at least r, then there exists Z∗⊆V (G) such that Z∗is (ξ∗, η∗)-centered in G and
intersects all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths in G contained in L.
The value η∗will be explicit when we formally prove it, but we omit it in this proof sketch
for simplicity. The finite graph case of Theorem 1.4 immediately follows from Theorem 2.1
(with the explicit description of η∗) by taking L = G and Z = ∅. The locally finite case of
Theorem 1.4 follows from Theorem 2.1 by using a compactness argument, and we will prove
it in Section 8.2. We remark that the bound for cH in Theorem 1.4 promised in Section 1
11


## Page 12


follows the bound for η∗in Theorem 2.1, except for the case that H is an apex-graph. To
obtain the bound cH ≤14 for Theorem 1.4, we will replace part of the proof of Theorem 2.1
by a simpler one to prove the apex-graph H case for Theorem 1.4 directly in Section 8.3.
So it suffices to sketch a proof of Theorem 2.1.
2.1
First step in the proof of Theorem 2.1
We shall prove Theorem 2.1 by induction on k (i.e. the maximum number of paths in
a packing).
The strategy is to find a set Z0 which is a union of a bounded number of
balls of bounded radius and find a bounded number of induced subgraphs L1, L2, ..., Lt of
G such that each Li does not contain k −1 (ℓ, X, Y )-paths in G and all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths in
G contained in L disjoint from Z0 are contained in one of L1, ..., Lt; so we can apply the
inductive hypothesis to each Li to obtain a bounded number of balls of bounded radius to
hit all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths contained in each Li, and hence the union of Z0 and those balls hits
all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths contained in L. Unlike many proofs in graph theory, we cannot just take
induced subgraphs to apply this strategy since the metric given by G is different from the
metric given by an induced subgraph. So we have to keep track the metric given by the
whole graph G. It is the reason why we consider both the graphs L and G in Theorem 2.1
instead of just the whole graph G like in Theorem 1.4. Moreover, we need the additional
technical assumption that NG(V (L)) is contained in a (ξ, η)-centered set in order to make
the rest of the argument work. Indeed, this additional technical assumption is natural based
on the how we will find the aforementioned induced subgraphs L1, L2, ..., Lt with smaller
packing number, which we will describe below.
A separation of a graph G is a pair (A, B) of edge-disjoint subgraphs such that A∪B = G.
In other words, no edge of G is between V (A)−V (B) and V (B)−V (A), so V (A∩B) separates
the graph; we call |V (A ∩B)| the order of (A, B).
If there exists a separation (A, B) of L of small order such that no (ℓ, X, Y )-path contained
in L is far away from V (A ∩B) ∪NG(V (L)), then the union of balls centered at V (A ∩
B) ∪NG(V (L)) with small radius hits all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths contained in L, and this union is
contained in a union of a bounded number of balls of small radius because NG(V (L)) ⊆Z
is (ξ, η)-centered, so we are done. Hence we may assume that for every separation (A, B) of
L of small order, A or B contains an (ℓ, X, Y )-path far away from V (A ∩B) ∪NG(V (L)). If
there exists a separation (A, B) of L of small order such that A contains an (ℓ, X, Y )-path
far away from V (A∩B)∪NG(V (L)), then B does not contain k −1 (ℓ, X, Y )-paths far away
from V (A∩B)∪NG(V (L)), for otherwise those k−1 (ℓ, X, Y )-paths contained in B together
with the one in A are k (ℓ, X, Y )-paths pairwise at distance at least r in G. If we additionally
know that B also contains an (ℓ, X, Y )-path far away from V (A∩B)∪NG(V (L)), then A also
does not contain k−1 (ℓ, X, Y )-paths far away from V (A∩B)∪NG(V (L)). In this situation,
a union of a small number of balls of small radius that contains V (A∩B)∪NG(V (L)) serves
the set Z0, and A −Z0 and B −Z0 serve the subgraphs L1, L2 in the argument in beginning
of this subsection and we are done.
Hence we may assume that for every separation (A, B) of L of small order, exactly one
of A and B contains an (ℓ, X, Y )-path that is far away from V (A ∩B) ∪NG(V (L)). In other
words, for every separation (A, B) of small order, exactly one of A and B is “important”.
This allows us to define a tangle, which is a fundamental notion in the Graph Minors series
12


## Page 13


of Robertson and Seymour. Roughly speaking, a tangle records the important side of each
separation of small order.
We will formally define tangles in Section 3.2 and formally prove that either we obtain a
desired hitting set for (ℓ, X, Y )-paths or we obtain a tangle, as sketched above. In fact, we
have to be careful when we implement this strategy in order to make sure that the bound
of the radius of balls in the hitting set is independent of the number k of the paths in the
packing.
The machinery developed in Section 3.2 is general and is expected to be applicable for
solving other problems related to the coarse Erd˝os-P´osa property about packing objects that
are pairwise far apart and covering objects by using a bounded number of balls of bounded
radius.
Moreover, the machinery we have sketched so far is sufficient to prove the case that H is
planar in Theorem 2.1 due to the Grid Minor Theorem of Robertson and Seymour. So we
may assume that H is non-planar from now on in the proof sketch.
2.2
Structure theorem
Robertson and Seymour’s structure theorem [45] (Theorem 3.10 in this paper) states
that every H-minor free graph has a “near embedding with respect to the given tangle”
in a surface in which H cannot be drawn. The formal definition of a near embedding is
complicated. In Sections 3.3-3.8, we will define necessary notions to formally define near
embeddings and prove simple lemmas related to those notions that will be used in the rest
of the proof.
Roughly speaking, a graph G is “near embeddable” in a surface Σ if we can first remove a
bounded number of vertices (called “apices”) from G, and then partition the rest of the graph
into edge-disjoint subgraphs A1, A2, ..An (for some integer n) such that all but a bounded
number of those Ai’s share at most three vertices with Aj for j ̸= i, and the “central piece” of
this partition can be “arranged” in Σ. Note that the partition {A1, A2, ..., An} is equivalent
to a set of separations {(A1, B1), (A2, B2), ..., (An, Bn)} with E(Ai) ∩E(Aj) = ∅for i ̸= j;
such a set of separations is called a location and will be formally defined in Section 3.4. We
also need a cyclic ordering of V (Ai ∩Bi) for each i in order to define a near embedding. Each
Ai equipped with such a cyclic ordering is called a “society”. Each of those Ai’s allowed to
have more than three vertices in V (Ai ∩Bi) is called a “vortex” and is required to satisfy
other structural requirements. Societies and vortices will be defined in Section 3.5. This
near embedding is “with respect to a given tangle” if for each non-vortex (Ai, Bi), Bi is the
important side of (Ai, Bi) defined by the tangle. The location {(Ai, Bi) : i ∈[n]} together
with the cyclic ordering of each V (Ai ∩Bi) is called a “segregation”, which will be defined
in Section 3.6. The “central piece” of a segregation {(Ai, Bi) : i ∈[n]} is the graph obtained
from Tn
i=1 Bi by adding edges to make V (Ai ∩Bi) a clique for each non-vortex (Ai, Bi) and
make V (Ai ∩Bi) the vertex-set of a cycle passing through its vertices according to the cyclic
ordering of V (Ai ∩Bi). This “central piece” can be “arranged” in Σ if it can be drawn in
Σ with no crossing such that the cycle given by each vortex bounds a disk in Σ. We will
formally define arrangements in Section 3.8 and define surfaces and drawings in Section 3.7.
It is known that none of the crucial ingredients (apices, surfaces, vortices and tangles)
in the definition of a near embedding can be dropped. The tangle produced in the proof of
13


## Page 14


Theorem 2.1 discussed in the previous subsection allows us to continue the proof of Theorem
2.1 by reducing the problem on H-minor free graphs to near embeddings. Proving Theorem
2.1 for near embeddings is the most technical part of this paper, and we will explain the
strategy in the rest of this section. The proof requires several tools proved in the literature
as well as new ones developed in this paper. Those existing and new tools will be proved in
the rest of Section 3.
2.3
Near embeddings without apices
In this subsection, we show how to prove Theorem 2.1 for near embeddings if there do
not exist apices; namely we do not need to remove vertices before partitioning the graph
when we define a near embedding. We will show how to deal with general near embeddings
in the next subsection.
Near embeddings with no apices are easier to deal with because topological constraints
of the embeddings restrict the metric of the graph. For example, if we can find a subdivision
of a θ × θ-grid for some large θ contained in the near embedding such that it is disjoint from
the vortices, and the drawing of this subdivision of a grid coming from the near embedding
has the property that all but possibly the infinite region of the grid is homeomorphic to a
disk, then for any integers i, j with r + ℓ≤i < j ≤θ −r −ℓand j −i ≥r, the distance
between Pi and Pj is at least r due to the planarity of the drawing, and the lengths of Pi and
Pj are at least max{r +ℓ, θ −2r} ≥ℓ, where for every α ∈[θ], the path Pα is the subdivision
of the path obtained from the α-th column of the grid by removing the first r rows and the
last r rows; so if many Pi’s are from X to Y , then we obtain k (ℓ, X, Y )-paths pairwise at
distance in G at least r. In fact, we only require a “grid-like” graph instead of a subdivision
of a grid to construct those (ℓ, X, Y )-paths. Using machinery developed in Section 3, we
can show that either we can find such a grid-like graph to complete the proof, or we can
modify the near embedding by sweeping some vertices in the surface (i.e. some vertices in
the “central piece”) into a bounded number of vortices to obtain a new near embedding in
the same surface such that all vertices in X are contained in vortices or all vertices in Y are
contained in vortices.
Hence, we may without loss of generality assume that all vertices in X are contained in
vortices. Results in the literature show that every vortex is “essentially of bounded tree-
width”, which roughly means that it can be viewed as a graph that has no tangle of large
order. Hence the argument in Section 2.1 can be used to show that for every vortex W, either
there are sufficiently many paths contained in W from X to the boundary of W pairwise at
distance at least r, or there exists a set ZW which is a union of a bounded number of balls
of bounded radius hitting all such paths. (In fact, we will prove a variant of this argument
in Section 3.4, which is more flexible for future steps.)
We first assume that there exists a vortex such that we can find sufficiently many paths
from X to the boundary of this vortex. Then again we can use the machinery in Section 3
to show that either we can find a grid-like graph to obtain sufficiently many (ℓ, X, Y )-paths
that are pairwise far apart, or we can further sweep vertices in the surface into a bounded
number of vortices to create a new near embedding in the same surface such that all vertices
in X ∪Y are contained in vortices. The former completes the proof, so we may assume
that the latter holds. We then again use results in Section 3.4 to show that for each vortex,
14


## Page 15


either there exist at least k (ℓ, X, Y )-paths pairwise at distance at least r, or there exists a
bounded number of balls of bounded radius hitting all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths with an end in this
vortex. We complete the proof once the former holds for some vortex, so we may assume
that the latter holds for all vortices. But every vertex in X ∪Y is contained in some vortex,
so the union of the hitting set of each vortex is a hitting set for all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths, and the
proof is complete.
Hence we may assume that for every vortex W, there exists a set ZW which is a union of
a bounded number of balls of bounded radius hitting all paths in W from X to the boundary
of W. Since all vertices in X are contained in the union of the vortices, we know that the
only (ℓ, X, Y )-paths disjoint from S
W ZW, where the union is over all vortices W, must be
contained in vortices. Therefore, the results in Section 3.4 again completes the proof.
We will provide a formal proof for near embeddings with no apices in Sections 5-7. The
machinery developed in Sections 5-7 is in fact more general and technical because we will
use them to deal with the more general situation coming from the apices sketched in the
next subsection.
2.4
Handling apices
Finally we show how to deal with general near embeddings. The main challenge is that
apices can have neighbors everywhere in the graph, so the metric is no longer restricted by
the topological constraint given by surfaces. We remark that if H is an apex-graph, then
existing results in the literature imply that all the neighbors of the apices are essentially
contained in vortices, so the metric remains under control by the topological constraint.
Hence the strategy stated in the previous subsection is essentially sufficient to prove the
stronger form of the apex-graph H case of Theorem 1.4 so that the bound for the radius of
balls in the hitting set is an absolute constant.
Now we assume that the near embedding has a bounded number of apices, but there is
no constraint for the neighbors of the apices. We remove the balls of radius r centered at
the apices from L to create a smaller induced subgraph L′. If we can find a hitting set for
all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths contained in L′, then we can obtain a hitting set of all (ℓ, X, Y )-paths
contained in L by adding those balls centered at apices. So it suffices to work with L′.
Note that we can obtain a near embedding for L′ by restricting the near embedding for L.
So we obtain a near embedding with no apices, and it looks like we can apply the strategy
in the previous subsection.
However, there several issues.
First, the number of vertices
removed from L to obtain L′ is unbounded, so the tangle for L′ obtained in the first step
of the proof is different from the one for L. Second, even though the near embedding for L
represents the essence of the tangle for L, the near embedding for L′ obtained by restricting
the near embedding for L possibly does not present the essence of the tangle for L′, which
forbids us to apply machinery developed in Section 3 to implement the strategy sketched in
Section 2.3. Third, the topological constraint provided by the near embedding for L′ does
not restrict the metric in G because there might be a short-cut between two vertices in L′
by using vertices in L −V (L′).
The first issue is not hard to resolve because the tangle in L′ can create a tangle in L.
To deal with the third issue, we not only consider a near embedding of L′ but also
“remember” how vertices in L −V (L′) could create short-cuts between vertices in L′. Note
15


## Page 16


that the main reason why we worry about short-cuts is that two paths that are at distance in
L′ greater than r could be at distance in G at most r, so the short-cuts we worry about have
length at most r. As we remove balls of radius r centered at apices, the short-cuts we worry
about are disjoint from the apices and hence are restricted by the topological constraints
coming from the near embedding of L. So it is possible to “remember” those short-cuts in
a near embedding of L′. It is essentially the motivation of the “protected arrangements”
defined in Section 3.10.
The second issue is the most complicated to resolve.
In order to apply the strategy
sketched in Section 2.3, we require the near embedding to be informative enough to “rep-
resent” the tangle. So we have to “repair” the near embedding after we delete the balls
centered in the apices. As there could be an unbounded number of vertices in those balls,
it is possible that we have to further partition Ai into more subgraphs for an unbounded
number of the separations (Ai, Bi) in the location in the definition of a near embedding when
repairing the near embedding. So the modified near embedding is far from a subgraph of the
original near embedding. We also require the drawing of the “central piece” of the location
to have high representativity in the surface in order to apply machinery developed in Section
3. So we possibly have to further remove a bounded number of balls of bounded radius from
L′ to obtain an even smaller graph L′′ and modify the near embedding of L′′ to make it
embedded in a surface of smaller Euler genus, while still remembering the information of
vertices in the short-cuts between vertices in L′′ like we mentioned in the previous paragraph.
This repairing process is technical, and a large portion of the new lemmas proved in Section
3 is due to this step.
We present a formal proof for handling these issues in Section 4. In Section 8.1, we
combine all we have discussed so far to prove Theorem 2.1.
3
Preparation
3.1
Notation
We introduce notation that will be used in this paper.
We denote the set of positive integers by N and denote the set of nonnegative integers
by N0. For every t ∈N, we denote the set {1, 2, ..., t} by [t].
Let G be a graph.
For every S ⊆V (G), we define NG[S] = N ≤1
G [S] and NG(S) =
NG[S] −S. Let L be a subgraph of G, and let F be a set of subgraphs of G. We define
F ∩L to be the set {F ∈F : F ⊆L}. For every Z ⊆V (G), we define F −Z = F ∩(G−Z),
which is the set of members of F disjoint from Z.
3.2
Tangles
We show how to use tangles to deal with coarse Erd˝os-P´osa type problems in this section.
Let G be a graph. A separation of a graph G is a pair (A, B) of edge-disjoint subgraphs
with A ∪B = G; the order of (A, B) is |V (A) ∩V (B)|. A tangle T in G of order θ is a set
of separations of G, each of order less than θ such that
(T1) for every separation (A, B) of G of order less than θ, either (A, B) ∈T or (B, A) ∈T ;
16


## Page 17


(T2) if (A1, B1), (A2, B2), (A3, B3) ∈T , then A1 ∪A2 ∪A3 ̸= G;
(T3) if (A, B) ∈T , then V (A) ̸= V (G).
Lemma 3.1. Let G be a graph, and let L be an induced subgraph of G. Let F be a set of

We will use the following special case of Lemma 3.2.
Lemma 3.3. Let G be a graph, and let L be an induced subgraph of G. Let F be a set
of connected subgraphs of L. Let k, θ ∈N and r, r′, ξ, η ∈R≥0 with r′ ≥r/2. Let Z be

contain k members of F pairwise at distance in G at least r, then there exist Z∗⊆V (G)
with N ≤r′

G [Z] ∪V (L) and a (possibly empty) set H of disjoint connected

H contains every component C of L −Z∗with V (H) ∩V (C) ̸= ∅.
Proof. Apply Lemma 3.2 by taking (θi : i ∈[k]) to be the constant sequence whose every
entry equals θ. Then Statements 1,2,3 of this lemma follows from Statements 3,2,1 of Lemma
3.2, respectively.
By a version of the well-known Grid Minor Theorem (such as Theorems [46, (1.5)] and
[46, (2.2)]), for every planar graph H, there exists θ ∈N such that no H-minor free graph has
a tangle of order at least θ. Hence if the graph G is H-minor free for some planar graph H,
and θ is a sufficiently large integer, then the set H in the conclusion of Lemma 3.3 must be

We introduce other notions related to tangles that will be used in this paper.
Let T be a tangle in a graph G. For Z ⊆V (G) with |Z| < θ, we define T −Z to be
the set of all separations (A′, B′) of G −Z of order less than θ −|Z| such that there exists
(A, B) ∈T with Z ⊆V (A ∩B), A′ = A −Z and B′ = B −Z. It is proved in [41, Theorem
8.5] that T −Z is a tangle in G −Z of order θ −|Z|.
Let θ be a positive integer. A pretangle of order θ in a graph G is a set of separations of
G of order less than θ satisfying (T1) and (T2).
3.3
Minors and conformal tangles
Let H be a graph. An H-minor in a graph G is a function µ with domain V (H) ∪E(H)
such that
• for every v ∈V (H), µ(v) is a non-empty connected subgraph of G, called the branch
set for v,
• µ(u) ∩µ(v) = ∅for all distinct u, v ∈V (H),
• for every edge uv ∈E(H), µ(uv) is an edge of G between µ(u) and µ(v), and
• if e1 and e2 are distinct edges, then µ(e1) ̸= µ(e2).
A tangle T in G controls an H-minor µ if µ is an H-minor in G such that there do not exist
(A, B) ∈T of order less than |V (H)| and h ∈V (H) such that V (µ(h)) ⊆V (A).
Let G and H be graphs. Let TH be a pretangle in H of order θ ≥2. Let µ be an H-minor
in G. Let TG be the set of separations (A, B) of G of order less than θ such that there exists
(A′, B′) ∈TH with µ(E(A′)) = E(A) ∩µ(E(H)). By [41, (6.1)], if TH is a tangle, then TG is
a tangle in G of order θ. The same argument shows that TG is a pretangle in G of order θ.
We call TG the (pre)tangle induced by TH. We say that TH is conformal with a tangle T in
G if TG ⊆T .
3.4
Tree-decompositions and locations
Now we show how to use tree-decompositions to deal with coarse Erd˝os-P´osa property.
Tree-decompositions allow us to encode subgraphs into trees. Before stating related notions,
we first mention some results about trees in the literature.
Theorem 3.4 ([39, (8.7)]). Let T be a tree, and let F be a family of subtrees of T. Then for
any integer k ≥0, either there are k disjoint members of F, or there is a subset X ⊆V (T)
with |X| ≤k −1 intersecting all members of F.
Theorem 3.5 ([39, (8.6)]). Let T be a tree, and let F1, F2, ..., Fm be families of subtrees of T,
where m is a positive integer. Let x1, x2, ..., xm ≥0 be integers, and let k = x1 +x2 +...+xm.
If for every i ∈[m], Fi contains k disjoint members, then for every i ∈[m], there exist xi
members T i
1, T i
2, ..., T i
xi of Fi such that the members of {T α
β : α ∈[m], β ∈[xα]} are pairwise
disjoint.

Let G be a graph. A tree-decomposition of G is a pair (T, X), where T is a tree and
X = {Xt : t ∈V (T)} such that the following hold.
• S
t∈V (T) Xt = V (G).
• For every edge e of G, there exists t ∈V (T) such that Xt contains all ends of e.
• For every vertex v of G, the subgraph of T induced by the set {t ∈V (T) : v ∈Xt} is
connected.
For every t ∈V (T), the set Xt is called the bag at t. The adhesion of (T, X) is sup{|Xt∩Xt′| :
tt′ ∈E(T)}.
We say that a tree-decomposition (T, X) is a path-decomposition if T is path.
A location in a graph G is a set L of separations such that A ⊆B′ and A′ ⊆B for any
distinct (A, B), (A′, B′) ∈L. Let L be a location in a graph G. A tree-decomposition of
L is a tree-decomposition of G[T
(A,B)∈L V (B)] such that for every (A, B) ∈L, there exists
t ∈V (T) such that V (A ∩B) ⊆Xt.
Let F be a set of subgraphs of G. We say that F is component exchangeable if
• there exists c ∈N such that every member of F has exactly c components, and
• for every F ∈F, the c components of F can be denoted by F(1), F(2), ..., F(c)
such that Sc
i=1 Fi(i) ∈F for any (not necessarily distinct) F1, F2, ..., Fc ∈F with
F1(1), F2(2), ..., Fc(c) pairwise disjoint.
Note that if all members of F are connected, then F is component exchangeable. If W is a
graph with c components, then the set of all W-minors in G is component exchangeable. The
set of rooted ℓ-fat W-minor models is not component exchangeable, but it can be slightly
modified to make it component exchangeable, and our next lemma (Lemma 3.6) can still be
applied (see Proposition 3.7).

3.5
Societies and vortices
A society is a pair (S, Ω), where S is a graph and Ωis a cyclic ordering of a subset Ωof
V (S). Let Z ⊆V (S). We define
(S, Ω) −Z = (S −Z, Ω−Z),
where Ω−Z is the cyclic ordering obtained from Ωby deleting all vertices in Ω∩Z. We
define
(S, Ω)[Z] = (S[Z], Ω[Z]),
where Ω[Z] = Ω−(Ω−Z).
Let (S, Ω) be a society. We say (S, Ω) is effective if for every v ∈Ω, there exist |Ω| −1
paths in S from v to Ω−{v} only sharing v. Note that if (S, Ω) is effective and |Ω| ≤3,
then there exists a K|Ω|-minor in S such that each branch set contains exactly one element
of Ω.
There are simple ways to split a non-effective societies into a set of effective societies. We
state one way here. An adaption of a society (S, Ω) with |Ω| ≤3 is a set of societies defined
recursively as follows:
• If (S, Ω) is effective, then A = {(S, Ω)};
• if |Ω| = 2 and there exists no path in S between the two vertices, say a, b, in Ω,
then A = {(A, {a}), (B, {b})}, where (A, B) is a separation of S of order 0 such that
a ∈V (A) and b ∈V (B);
• if |Ω| = 3 and there exists a ∈Ωsuch that no path in S is from a to Ω−{a}, then
A = {(A, {a})} ∪A′, where (A, B) is a separation of S of order 0 such that a ∈V (A)
and Ω−{a} ⊆V (B), and A′ is an adaption of the society (B, Ω−{a});
• if |Ω| = 3, some component of S contains all vertices in Ω, but there exists a ∈Ω
such that there exist no two paths in S from a to Ω−{a} only sharing a, then
A = {(A, {a} ∪V (A ∩B))} ∪A′, where
26


## Page 27


– (A, B) is a separation of S of order 1 such that a ∈V (A) and there exist |Ω−
({a}∪V (A∩B))| paths in B from V (A∩B) to Ω−({a}∪V (A∩B)) only sharing
the vertex in V (A ∩B), and
– A′ is an adaption of the society (B, Ω′), where Ω′ is a cyclic ordering on V (A ∩
B) ∪(Ω−{a}) such that Ω′ −V (A ∩B) = Ω−{a}.
Note that the separations (A, B) mentioned above exist by Menger’s theorem. Moreover, all
societies in an adaption of (S, Ω) are effective.
Lemma 3.8. Let (S, Ω) be a society with |Ω| ≤3. If |Ω| ≤2, then every adaption of (S, Ω)
has size at most max{|Ω|, 1}. If |Ω| = 3, then every adaption of (S, Ω) has size at most 1+t,
where t is the number of vertices x ∈Ωsuch that no two paths in S from x to Ω−{x} only
sharing x.
Proof. Let A be an adaption of (S, Ω). If (S, Ω) is effective, then |A| = 1. So we may
assume that (S, Ω) is not effective. Hence |Ω| ≥2. If |Ω| = 2, then |A| = 2 by definition and
the lemma holds.
So we may assume |Ω| = 3 and denote the vertices in Ωby {a, b, c}. Let t be the number
of vertices x ∈Ωsuch that no two paths in S from x to Ω−{x} only sharing x. We shall
prove this lemma by induction on t.
When t = 0, (S, Ω) is effective, so |A| = 1. So we may assume t ≥1 and assume that
the lemma holds when t is smaller.
If no component of S contains all a, b, c, then there exists no path in S from some vertex
in Ω, say a, to the other two vertices in Ω, so A is a union of a set of size 1 and an adaption
A′ of a society (S′, Ω−{a}); since |Ω−{a}| ≤2, |A| = 1 + |A′| ≤3; note that every vertex
x ∈Ωsatisfies that no two paths in S from x to Ω−{x} only sharing x, so t = 3.
Hence we may assume that some component of S contains all a, b, c. Since t ≥1, by the
definition of A, there exist a ∈Ωand a separation (A, B) of S of order 1 such that a ∈V (A)
and there exist |{b, c} −V (A ∩B))| paths in B from V (A ∩B) to {b, c} −V (A ∩B)) only
sharing the vertex in V (A ∩B), and |A| = 1 + |A′|, where A′ is an adaption of the society
(B, Ω′), where Ω′ is a cyclic ordering on V (A∩B)∪{b, c} such that Ω′−V (A∩B) = Ω−{a}.
If |{b, c} ∪V (A ∩B))| = 2, then |Ω′| = 2 and (B, Ω′) is effective since some component
contains {a, b, c}, so |A| = 1 + |A′| = 2 ≤1 + t.
So we may assume |{b, c} ∪V (A ∩B))| = 3. Let v be the vertex in V (A ∩B). Note that
v ̸∈{b, c}. Moreover, for every u ∈{b, c}, if there exist two paths in S from u to Ω−{u}
only sharing u, then there exist two paths in B from u to {v} ∪(Ω−{a, u}) only sharing
u. Since there exist two paths in B from v to {b, c} only sharing v, the number of vertices
x ∈Ωsuch that no two paths in B from x to Ω′ −{x} only sharing x is at most t−1. Hence
|A| = 1 + |A′| ≤1 + (1 + (t −1)) ≤1 + t by the inductive hypothesis.
Let ρ be a nonnegative integer. A society (S, Ω) is a ρ-vortex if for all distinct u, v ∈Ω,
there do not exist ρ + 1 mutually disjoint paths in S between I ∪{u} and J ∪{v}, where I
is the set of vertices in Ωafter u and before v in Ω, and J is the set of vertices in Ωafter v
and before u in Ω.
27


## Page 28


Let (S, Ω) be a society with Ω= {v1, v2, ..., v|Ω|} in order. A vortical decomposition of
(S, Ω) is a path-decomposition (t1t2...t|Ω|, (Xti : 1 ≤i ≤|Ω|)) of S such that vi ∈Xti for
1 ≤i ≤|Ω|. The following theorem ensures the existence of a vortical decomposition.
Theorem 3.9 ([40, (8.1)]). For every positive integer ρ, every ρ-vortex has a vortical de-
composition of adhesion at most ρ.
3.6
Segregations
A segregation of a graph G is a set S of societies such that the following conditions hold.
• S is a subgraph of G for every (S, Ω) ∈S, and S
(S,Ω)∈S S = G.
• V (S ∩S′) ⊆Ω∩Ω′ and E(S ∩S′) = ∅for any distinct (S, Ω) and (S′, Ω′) ∈S.
We write V (S) = S
(S,Ω)∈S Ω. For a tangle T in G, we say that a segregation S of G is
T -central if for every (S, Ω) ∈S, there is no (A, B) ∈T of order at most half of the order
of T with B ⊆S.
Let κ, ρ be nonnegative integers.
For subsets S1, S2 of a segregation S, we say that
(S1, S2) is a (κ, ρ)-witness of S if S1 ∪S2 = S, S1 ∩S2 = ∅, |Ω| ≤3 for every (S, Ω) ∈S1,
|S2| ≤κ and every member of S2 is a ρ-vortex. (Note that we do not require that |Ω| > 3 for
every member (S, Ω) ∈S2.) We say that S is a (κ, ρ)-segregation if it has a (κ, ρ)-witness.
Let S be a segregation of a graph G. Let S1, S2 be subsets of S with S1 ∩S2 = ∅and
S1 ∪S2 = S such that |Ω| ≤3 for every (S, Ω) ∈S1. We say S is effective with respect to
(S1, S2) if every member of S1 is effective.
Let S be a segregation of a graph G. Let Z ⊆V (G), and let S′ ⊆S. Then we define
S′[Z] = {(S, Ω)[Z] : (S, Ω) ∈S′}.
Note that S[Z] is a segregation of G[Z].

L+ = G[N ≤r
G [V (L)]]. Let S+ be a segregation of L+ with a (κ, ρ)-witness (S+

κ, ρ ∈N0. An (L, S+

3.7
Surfaces and drawings
In this paper, a surface is a nonnull compact 2-manifold without boundary. For every
subset ∆of a surface Σ, we denote the closure of ∆by ∆, and the boundary of ∆by ∂∆.
An O-arc in a surface Σ is a subset of Σ homeomorphic to a circle. A line in a surface Σ is
a subset of Σ homeomorphic to the interval [0, 1].
A drawing in a surface Σ is a pair (U, V ), where V ⊆U ⊆Σ, U is closed, V is finite,
U −V has only finitely many arc-wise connected components, called edges, and for every
edge e, either the closure ¯e of e is a line whose set of ends is ¯e ∩V , or ¯e is an O-arc and
28


## Page 29


|¯e ∩V | = 1. Every component of Σ −U is called a region. Every member of V is called a
vertex. For a drawing Γ = (U, V ), we define U(Γ) = U, V (Γ) = V , and define the set of
edges to be E(Γ).
Let Σ be a surface and Γ a drawing in Σ. For a vertex v of Γ and an edge or a region
e of Γ, we say that e is incident with v if v ∈e. The incidence relation between V (Γ) and
E(Γ) defines a graph, and we say that Γ is a drawing of G in Σ if G is a graph defined by
this incidence relation. In this case, we say that G is embeddable in Σ, or G can be drawn
in Σ. An atom of Γ is an edge of Γ, a region of Γ, or a set {v} for some v ∈V (Γ).
Let Σ be a surface and Γ a drawing in Σ. A drawing Γ′ is a subdrawing of Γ if V (Γ′) ⊆
V (Γ) and E(Γ′) ⊆E(Γ). We write Γ′ ⊆Γ if Γ′ is a subdrawing of Γ. For a closed set ∆⊆Σ
with either ¯e ⊆∆or e ∩∆= ∅for each e ∈E(Γ), we define Γ ∩∆to be the drawing
(U(Γ) ∩∆, V (Γ) ∩∆).
Let Σ be a surface and Γ a drawing in Σ. A subset Z of Σ is Γ-normal if Z∩U(Γ) ⊆V (Γ).
If Σ is connected and not a sphere, we say that Γ is θ-representative if |F ∩V (Γ)| ≥θ for
every non-null-homotopic Γ-normal O-arc F in Σ. We say Γ is 2-cell if Σ is connected and
every region of Γ is an open disk.
Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface Σ. We say that a drawing K in Σ is a radial
drawing of Γ if it satisfies the following.
• U(Γ) ∩U(K) = V (Γ) ⊆V (K).
• Each region r of Γ contains a unique vertex of K.
• K is a drawing of a bipartite graph, and (V (Γ), V (K) −V (Γ)) is its bipartition.
• For every v ∈V (Γ), the edges of K ∪Γ incident with v belong alternately to Γ and to
K (in their cyclic order around v).
3.8
Arrangements
Let Σ be a surface and S = {(S1, Ω1), ..., (Sk, Ωk)} a segregation of G. An arrangement
of S in Σ is a function α with domain S ∪V (S), such that the following conditions hold.
• For 1 ≤i ≤k, α(Si, Ωi) is a closed disk ∆i ⊆Σ, and α(x) ∈∂∆i for each x ∈Ωi.
• For 1 ≤i < j ≤k, if x ∈∆i ∩∆j, then x = α(v) for some v ∈Ωi ∩Ωj.
• For all distinct x, y ∈V (S), α(x) ̸= α(y).
• For 1 ≤i ≤k, Ωi is mapped by α to a natural order of α(Ωi) determined by ∂∆i.
An arrangement is proper if ∆i ∩∆j = ∅for all 1 ≤i < j ≤k with |Ωi|, |Ωj| > 3.
We will use the following celebrated structure theorem of Robertson and Seymour.
Theorem 3.10 ([45, (3.1)]). For every graph H, there exist nonnegative integers κ, ρ, ξ and
a positive integer θ such that if T is a tangle in a graph G of order at least θ controlling
no H-minor of G, then there exist Z ⊆V (G) with |Z| ≤ξ and a (T −Z)-central (κ, ρ)-
segregation of G −Z which has a proper arrangement in a surface in which H cannot be
drawn.
29


## Page 30

3.9
Skeletons
Let S be a segregation of a graph G. Let S1, S2 be subsets of S with S1 ∩S2 = ∅and
S1 ∪S2 = S such that |Ω| ≤3 for every (S, Ω) ∈S1. Let α be a proper arrangement of
S in a surface Σ. The skeleton of α with respect to (S1, S2) is the drawing Γ = (U, V ) in
Σ with V (Γ) = {α(v) : v ∈V (S)} such that U(Γ) consists of the boundary of α(S, Ω) for
each (S, Ω) ∈S1 with |Ω| = 3, and a line in the boundary of α(S′, Ω′) with ends Ω′ for each
(S′, Ω′) ∈S1 with |Ω′| = 2. Note that no edge of the skeleton is created by (S, Ω) ∈S1 with
|Ω| ≤1 or by (S, Ω) ∈S2. It is easy to see that if S is an effective segregation with respect
to (S1, S2), then the skeleton of α with respect to (S1, S2) is a minor of G; more precisely,
there exists an H-minor µ in G, where H is the skeleton of α with respect to (S1, S2), such
that
• for every v ∈V (H), µ(v) is a subgraph containing vG, where vG is the vertex of G with
α(vG) = v, and
• for every e ∈E(H), µ(e) is an edge of Se, where (Se, Ωe) is the member of S1 such that
e is contained in ∂α(Se, Ωe);
we say that the skeleton of α with respect to (S1, S2) is a natural minor of G if such µ exists.
Now we show how to use Lemma 3.6 to find a hitting set for objects contained in vortices.

3.10
Protected arrangements
Given an effective segregation S of an induced subgraph L of a graph G, we can “project”
every path in L to a path in the skeleton L′ of S. So given two vertices u, v in L, distL(u, v)
can be somehow told from the distance between some vertices in L′. But we have to consider
distG(u, v) instead of distL(u, v). So we need some notions to deal with paths in G between
two vertices in L.
Let G be a graph, and let L be an induced subgraph of G. Let S be a segregation of L
with a (κ, ρ)-witness (S1, S2) for some integers κ, ρ. Let c, r be positive integers, and let Σ
be a surface. Let α be an arrangement of S in Σ. Let L′ be the skeleton of α with respect
to (S1, S2), and let K be a radial drawing of L′. We say that α is (c, r, S1, S2)-protected if
for any u, v ∈V (L) with distG(u, v) ≤r, there exists a path in L′ ∪K with at most cr edges
between Ωu and Ωv for some (Su, Ωu) and (Sv, Ωv) ∈S with u ∈V (Su) and v ∈V (Sv).

3.11
Respectful tangles
Let Σ be a surface, and let Γ be a drawing of a graph G in Σ. A tangle (or pretangle) in
Γ and a separation of Γ are a tangle (or pretangle) in G and a separation of G, respectively.
A tangle (or pretangle, respectively) T in Γ of order θ is respectful (towards Σ) if Σ is
connected, and for every Γ-normal O-arc F in Σ with |F ∩V (Γ)| < θ, there is a closed disk
∆⊆Σ with ∂∆= F such that (Γ ∩∆, Γ ∩Σ −∆) ∈T ; since T satisfies (T1), the disk ∆
is unique, and we write ∆= ins(F); the function ins is called the inside function of T .
Theorem 3.13 ([42, (4.1)]). Let Σ be a connected surface, not a sphere. Let θ ≥1, and let
Γ be a θ-representative 2-cell drawing in Σ. Then there is a unique respectful tangle in Γ of
order θ.
Let Γ be a drawing in a connected surface Σ. Let θ ≥1 such that 2θ is an integer. A
slope in Γ of order θ is a function f that assigns each cycle C in Γ of length less than 2θ a
closed disk f(C) in Σ bounded by U(C) such that
• if C1 and C2 are cycles in Γ of length less than 2θ such that U(C1) ⊆f(C2), then
f(C1) ⊆f(C2), and
• if P0, P1, P2 are three non-trivial pairwise disjoint paths in Γ with same ends such that
the cycles P0 ∪P1, P1 ∪P2 and P2 ∪P0 have length less than 2θ, then one of the three
disks f(P0 ∪P1), f(P1 ∪P2), f(P2 ∪P0) contains the other two.
Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface Σ, and let K be a radial drawing of Γ. Let T be a
respectful pretangle in Γ of order θ for some integer θ. For a closed walk W of K, we define
K|W to be the subdrawing of K formed by the vertices and the edges in W; note that if
C is a cycle of K|W of length less than 2θ, then C is a Γ-normal O-arc in Σ and ins(C) is
defined by the inside function of T . For every closed walk W of K of length less than 2θ,
we define ins(W) to be the union of U(K|W) and ins(C), taken over all cycles C of K|W.
Note that the restriction of ins on the set of all cycles of K of length less than 2θ is a slope
of order θ, and we call it the slope derived from T .
The following is a consequence of [42, (6.3)] that provides a membership test for a pre-
tangle T in terms of the slope derived from T .
Theorem 3.14 ([42, (6.3)]). Let Σ be a connected surface, and let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in
Σ with E(Γ) ̸= ∅. Let K be a radial drawing of Γ in Σ. Let T be a respectful pretangle of
order θ ≥1 in Γ toward Σ, and let f be the slope derived from T . Let (A, B) be a separation
of Γ of order less than θ. Then (A, B) ∈T if and only if for every e ∈E(A), there exists a
cycle C of K with V (C) ∩V (Γ) ⊆V (A ∩B)5 and with e ⊆f(C).
5In [42, (6.3)], it was stated as V (C) ⊆V (Γ) ⊆V (A∩B), but we believe that it should be V (C)∩V (Γ) ⊆
V (A ∩B).

We have seen that a pretangle can derive a slope. The converse statement is also true in
the sense that a slope can define a pretangle, as shown by the following simpler form of [42,
(6.1)].
Theorem 3.15 ([42, (6.1)]). Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a connected surface Σ with E(Γ) ̸=
∅, and let K be a radial drawing of Γ. Let θ ≥1 be an integer, and let f be a slope in K of
order θ. Then there exists a respectful pretangle T in Γ of order θ such that f is the slope
derived from T .
Now we prove the key lemma of this subsection.

3.12
A metric in a drawing and zones
Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface Σ, and let K be a radial drawing of Γ. Let T be a
respectful tangle in Γ of order θ for some integer θ, and let ins be the inside function of T
For any two atoms a, b of K, define a function mT (a, b) as follows:
• if a = b, then mT (a, b) = 0;
• if a ̸= b and a, b ⊆ins(W) for some closed walk W of K of length less than 2θ, then
mT (a, b) = min 1
2|E(W)|, taking over all such closed walks W;
• otherwise, mT (a, b) = θ.
Since K is bipartite, mT is integral. In addition, for every atom c of Γ, we define a(c) to be
an atom of K such that
• a(c) = c if c ⊆V (Γ);
• a(c) is the region of K including c if c is an edge of Γ;
• a(c) = {v}, where v is the vertex of K in c, if c is a region of Γ.
For atoms b, c of Γ, we define mT (b, c) = mT (a(b), a(c)). Note that [42, (9.1)] implies that
the following:
Theorem 3.17 ([42]). Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface Σ. If T is a respectful tangle
in Γ, then mT is a metric on the atoms of Γ.
Let T be a respectful tangle in a 2-cell drawing G in a surface Σ. If X, Y are sets of
atoms of G, then we define mT (X, Y ) = min{mT (x, y) : x ∈X, y ∈Y }. When one of X and
Y , say Y , has size one, we denote mT (X, Y ) by mT (X, y), where y is the unique element of
Y .
The following is a restatement of a result in [33].
Theorem 3.18 ([33, Theorem 5.3]). Let Σ be a connected surface, and let Γ be a 2-cell
drawing of a graph in Σ with E(Γ) ̸= ∅.
Let T be a respectful tangle of order θ in Γ.
Let x ∈V (Γ). If (A, B) ∈T is a separation of Γ such that x ∈V (A) −V (B) and there
exists a path P in A from x to a vertex y ∈V (A) internally disjoint from V (B), then
mT (x, y) ≤|V (A) ∩V (B)|.
We also need the following variant:
Lemma 3.19 ([32, Lemma 5.2]). Let Σ be a surface, and let Γ be a 2-cell drawing of a
graph in Σ with E(Γ) ̸= ∅. Let T be a respectful tangle of order θ in Γ. Let X ⊆V (Γ). If
(A, B) ∈T is a separation of Γ of order less than |X| such that X ⊆V (A), and subject to
this, A is minimal, then mT (X, y) ≤|V (A) ∩V (B)| for every y ∈V (A).

## Page 39


Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface Σ, and let T be a respectful tangle of order θ in
Γ. Let x be an atom of Γ. A λ-zone around x is an open disk ∆in Σ with x ⊆∆such
that ∂∆is an O-arc, ∂∆⊆U(Γ), mT (x, y) ≤λ for every atom y of Γ with y ⊆∆, and if
x ∈E(Γ), then λ ≥2. A λ-zone is a λ-zone around some atom. Let ∆be a λ-zone. Note
that U(Γ) ∩∂∆is a cycle, and the drawing Γ′ = Γ ∩(Σ −∆) is 2-cell in Σ. We say that Γ′
is the drawing obtained from Γ by clearing ∆. We say that T ′ is a tangle of order θ −4λ −2
obtained by clearing ∆if T ′ is a tangle in Γ′ of order θ −4λ −2 satisfying the following.
• T ′ is respectful with a metric mT ′.
• T ′ is conformal with T .
• If x, y are atoms of Γ and x′, y′ are atoms of Γ′ with x ⊆x′ and y ⊆y′, then mT (x, y) ≥
mT ′(x′, y′) ≥mT (x, y) −4λ −2.
Theorem 3.20 ([43, (7.10)]). Let ∆be a λ-zone. If θ ≥4λ + 3, then there exists a unique
respectful tangle of order θ −4λ −2 obtained by clearing ∆.
Theorem 3.21 ([44, (9.2)]). Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface Σ, and let T be a
respectful tangle in Γ of order θ. Let x be an atom of Γ, and λ an integer with 2 ≤λ ≤θ −4.
Then there exists a (λ + 3)-zone ∆around x such that x′ ⊆∆for every atom x′ of Γ with
mT (x, x′) ≤λ.
Lemma 3.22 ([33, Lemma 5.7]). Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface, z an atom, and T
a respectful tangle in Γ of order θ. Let λ be a nonnegative integer, and let C be the cycle of
the boundary of a λ-zone around z. If θ ≥λ + 8, then there exists a (λ + 7)-zone Λ around z
such that the cycle bounding Λ is disjoint from C, and Λ contains the λ-zone bounded by C.
Lemma 3.23. Let Γ be a 2-cell drawing in a surface, z an atom of Γ, and T a respectful
tangle in Γ of order θ. Let λ and k be positive integers. Let Y be a subset of atoms of Γ
such that mT (z, y) ≤λ for every y ∈Y . If θ ≥10k + λ + 4, then there exist disjoint cycles
C1, C2, ..., Ck in Γ such that
1. for each i ∈[k], Ci bounds a (λ + 3 + 10i)-zone Λi around z,
2. Λk ⊇Λk−1 ⊇... ⊇Λ1 ⊇Y , and
3. x ⊆Λi for every atom x of Γ with mT (z, x) ≤λ + 10i for every i ∈[k].
Proof. By Theorem 3.21, there exists a (λ + 3)-zone Λ0 around z such that x ⊆Λ0 for
every atom x of Γ with mT (z, x) ≤λ. So Y ⊆Λ0. Let C0 be the cycle in Γ bounding Λ0.
For every i ∈[k], by Theorem 3.21 and Lemma 3.22, there exists a (λ + 3 + 10i)-zone Λi
around z such that the cycle Ci bounding Λi is disjoint from Ci−1, and Λi ⊇Λi−1 and x ⊆Λi
for every atom x of Γ with mT (z, x) ≤λ + 3 + 10(i −1) + 7. Then C1, C2, ..., Ck are cycles
satisfying this lemma.

3.13
Better arrangements
Let Σ be a surface, θ an integer, and ϕ a nondecreasing function with domain Z. Let T be
a tangle in a graph G. We say that an arrangement α of a segregation S of G with a (κ, ρ)-
witness (S1, S2) in Σ is a (Σ, θ, ϕ, T )-arrangement with respect to (S1, S2) if the following
conditions hold.
• α is a proper arrangement of S in Σ.
• The skeleton of α with respect to (S1, S2) is a 2-cell drawing in Σ and is a natural
minor of G.
• There exists a respectful tangle T ′ of order at least θ in the skeleton of α with respect
to (S1, S2) conformal with T .
• mT ′(Ω, Ω′) ≥ϕ(ρ′) for all distinct members (S, Ω), (S′, Ω′) of S2, where ρ′ is the smallest
nonnegative integer such that every member of S2 is a ρ′-vortex.
• S is a T -central segregation.
When ϕ is a constant function with ϕ(x) = c for some c, we also write a (Σ, θ, ϕ, T )-
arrangement as a (Σ, θ, c, T )-arrangement.
